Like cereal, crackers, peanut butter, whatever snacks I had packed for my classroom. It’s embarrassing to say, but the amount can really scare me.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating this large amount of food at night, do you feel like you can stop whenever you want?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’d like to think I could, but once I start, it’s like the sensible part of me—the teacher part that’s usually organized and in control—is kind of watching from the outside saying, “Joan, stop.” And I hear it, but my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth. It keeps going until I’m really, painfully full. I know that probably sounds awful.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food at night?\n\nPatient: Pretty ashamed, honestly. I usually feel like I need to… make up for it somehow. So I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to have breakfast or lunch at school the next day, which I know probably isn’t the best.\n\nAnd sometimes I’ll go out on the trail for hours, or do extra yoga, even if I’m shaky or really tired. I tell myself it’s just being disciplined, but… it feels more like punishment, I guess. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food at night?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been doing that, not in the way you’re asking. It’s more that I feel awful afterward and try to fix it in other ways.\n\nSorry, is that… is that what you meant?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to counteract the effects of your eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I know what those are, but that hasn’t really been one of the things I do to deal with it.\n\nI mostly just feel guilty and try to be “better” the next day, if that makes sense. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you have times where you eat a large amount of food in a short period, like you described, without doing anything to try to counteract it afterwards?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I guess I usually do something afterward, even if I try to tell myself it’s just “getting back on track.”\n\nLike after one of those nights, I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school, and then I might stay out on the trail for hours or do extra yoga, even when I’m shaky and exhausted. I know that probably sounds bad. I don’t always think of it as “counteracting” in the moment, but… I guess that’s what it is.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food at night happen?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s been happening more than I’d like to admit. For the past few months, I don’t think there’s really been many weeks where it didn’t happen at least once.\n\nUsually it’s one night where I eat way more than I meant to, and then the next day I try to sort of… make up for it, I guess, by not eating at school or making myself do a really long walk or extra yoga. I know that probably isn’t the best answer, but that’s what’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: How often do you do extra exercise or restrict your food intake to make up for one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food at night?\n\nPatient: Probably… most times it happens. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but for the past few months there’s barely been a week where I didn’t have at least one of those nights, and then the next day I usually try to “fix” it somehow.\n\nLike I’ll skip eating at school, or I’ll make myself go on a really long walk, or do extra yoga even if I’m tired. I know that’s probably not a great way to handle it… I’m just trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in these extra exercises or food restrictions every time you have one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food at night?\n\nPatient: Pretty much… yes. I don’t think I let myself just move on from it.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I’ll usually tell myself I’m not allowed to have breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll try to make up for it by staying out on the trail for hours or doing extra yoga. Even if I feel shaky or really exhausted, I still kind of push myself. I know that probably sounds like a lot… but yeah, it’s basically every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have weeks where you don't try to make up for one of these eating episodes with extra exercise or restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I wish I could say yes, but for the past few months there’s almost always been at least one night a week where I eat way more than I meant to.\n\nAnd then the next day I usually try to make up for it somehow — like not eating at school, or pushing myself to do a really long walk or extra yoga. I know that’s not ideal… I’m sorry, I’m trying to answer clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any days where you don't try to make up for one of these eating episodes with extra exercise or restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: I mean… there might be a day here or there where I don’t do it as much, but honestly, not really.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I usually tell myself I’m not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll try to make up for it by staying out on the trail for hours or doing extra yoga. Even when I’m shaky or exhausted, I still push it. I know that probably sounds bad… I’m just trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have a month where you don't try to make up for these eating episodes with extra exercise or restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a whole month without doing that.\n\nI guess I keep hoping it’s not as often as it sounds, but for the past few months there’s rarely even been a week where I don’t have at least one night where I eat way more than I meant to. And then the next day I usually try to make up for it somehow… like not eating while I’m at school, or making myself go for a really long walk or do extra yoga.\n\nI’m sorry — I know that’s probably not the answer you’d want, but I’m trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to make up for these eating episodes with extra exercise or restricting your food intake every single time they happen?\n\nPatient: Pretty much, yes… I mean, I don’t like saying “every single time” because maybe there’s been a time I didn’t do it perfectly, but after one of those nights I almost always try to make up for it.\n\nUsually I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to have breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll stay out on the trail for hours, or do extra yoga, even if I’m shaky or really tired. I know that sounds… not good. I’m sorry. But yes, that’s usually what happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It’s embarrassing to say, but the amount can really scare me.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating this large amount of food at night, do you feel like you can stop whenever you want?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’d like to think I could, but once I start, it’s like the sensible part of me—the teacher part that’s usually organized and in control—is kind of watching from the outside saying, “Joan, stop.” And I hear it, but my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth. It keeps going until I’m really, painfully full. I know that probably sounds awful.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food at night?\n\nPatient: Pretty ashamed, honestly. I usually feel like I need to… make up for it somehow. So I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to have breakfast or lunch at school the next day, which I know probably isn’t the best.\n\nAnd sometimes I’ll go out on the trail for hours, or do extra yoga, even if I’m shaky or really tired. I tell myself it’s just being disciplined, but… it feels more like punishment, I guess. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food at night?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been doing that, not in the way you’re asking. It’s more that I feel awful afterward and try to fix it in other ways.\n\nSorry, is that… is that what you meant?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to counteract the effects of your eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I know what those are, but that hasn’t really been one of the things I do to deal with it.\n\nI mostly just feel guilty and try to be “better” the next day, if that makes sense. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you have times where you eat a large amount of food in a short period, like you described, without doing anything to try to counteract it afterwards?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I guess I usually do something afterward, even if I try to tell myself it’s just “getting back on track.”\n\nLike after one of those nights, I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school, and then I might stay out on the trail for hours or do extra yoga, even when I’m shaky and exhausted. I know that probably sounds bad. I don’t always think of it as “counteracting” in the moment, but… I guess that’s what it is.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food at night happen?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s been happening more than I’d like to admit. For the past few months, I don’t think there’s really been many weeks where it didn’t happen at least once.\n\nUsually it’s one night where I eat way more than I meant to, and then the next day I try to sort of… make up for it, I guess, by not eating at school or making myself do a really long walk or extra yoga. I know that probably isn’t the best answer, but that’s what’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: How often do you do extra exercise or restrict your food intake to make up for one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food at night?\n\nPatient: Probably… most times it happens. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but for the past few months there’s barely been a week where I didn’t have at least one of those nights, and then the next day I usually try to “fix” it somehow.\n\nLike I’ll skip eating at school, or I’ll make myself go on a really long walk, or do extra yoga even if I’m tired. I know that’s probably not a great way to handle it… I’m just trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in these extra exercises or food restrictions every time you have one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food at night?\n\nPatient: Pretty much… yes. I don’t think I let myself just move on from it.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I’ll usually tell myself I’m not allowed to have breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll try to make up for it by staying out on the trail for hours or doing extra yoga. Even if I feel shaky or really exhausted, I still kind of push myself. I know that probably sounds like a lot… but yeah, it’s basically every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have weeks where you don't try to make up for one of these eating episodes with extra exercise or restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I wish I could say yes, but for the past few months there’s almost always been at least one night a week where I eat way more than I meant to.\n\nAnd then the next day I usually try to make up for it somehow — like not eating at school, or pushing myself to do a really long walk or extra yoga. I know that’s not ideal… I’m sorry, I’m trying to answer clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any days where you don't try to make up for one of these eating episodes with extra exercise or restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: I mean… there might be a day here or there where I don’t do it as much, but honestly, not really.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I usually tell myself I’m not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll try to make up for it by staying out on the trail for hours or doing extra yoga. Even when I’m shaky or exhausted, I still push it. I know that probably sounds bad… I’m just trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have a month where you don't try to make up for these eating episodes with extra exercise or restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a whole month without doing that.\n\nI guess I keep hoping it’s not as often as it sounds, but for the past few months there’s rarely even been a week where I don’t have at least one night where I eat way more than I meant to. And then the next day I usually try to make up for it somehow… like not eating while I’m at school, or making myself go for a really long walk or do extra yoga.\n\nI’m sorry — I know that’s probably not the answer you’d want, but I’m trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to make up for these eating episodes with extra exercise or restricting your food intake every single time they happen?\n\nPatient: Pretty much, yes… I mean, I don’t like saying “every single time” because maybe there’s been a time I didn’t do it perfectly, but after one of those nights I almost always try to make up for it.\n\nUsually I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to have breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll stay out on the trail for hours, or do extra yoga, even if I’m shaky or really tired. I know that sounds… not good. I’m sorry. But yes, that’s usually what happens.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to make up for these eating episodes because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t really want to admit that, but it is mostly because I’m scared I’ll gain weight after one of those nights.\n\nSo then the next day I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school, and I’ll try to “fix it” by staying out on the trail for hours or doing extra yoga, even when I’m shaky and exhausted. I know that probably isn’t a good way to handle it… I just get really anxious if I don’t do something.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because my eating has been getting a little out of hand. Especially at night. I’ll come home after teaching, grade papers, and then I start with something normal, like leftovers… but sometimes it turns into a lot more. Like cereal, crackers, peanut butter, whatever snacks I had packed for my classroom. It’s embarrassing to say, but the amount can really scare me.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating this large amount of food at night, do you feel like you can stop whenever you want?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’d like to think I could, but once I start, it’s like the sensible part of me—the teacher part that’s usually organized and in control—is kind of watching from the outside saying, “Joan, stop.” And I hear it, but my hands just keep opening things and putting food in my mouth. I know that probably sounds bad. I don’t always think of it as “counteracting” in the moment, but… I guess that’s what it is.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food at night happen?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s been happening more than I’d like to admit. For the past few months, I don’t think there’s really been many weeks where it didn’t happen at least once.\n\nUsually it’s one night where I eat way more than I meant to, and then the next day I try to sort of… make up for it, I guess, by not eating at school or making myself do a really long walk or extra yoga. I know that probably isn’t the best answer, but that’s what’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: How often do you do extra exercise or restrict your food intake to make up for one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food at night?\n\nPatient: Probably… most times it happens. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but for the past few months there’s barely been a week where I didn’t have at least one of those nights, and then the next day I usually try to “fix” it somehow.\n\nLike I’ll skip eating at school, or I’ll make myself go on a really long walk, or do extra yoga even if I’m tired. I know that’s probably not a great way to handle it… I’m just trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in these extra exercises or food restrictions every time you have one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food at night?\n\nPatient: Pretty much… yes. I don’t think I let myself just move on from it.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I’ll usually tell myself I’m not allowed to have breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll try to make up for it by staying out on the trail for hours or doing extra yoga. Even if I feel shaky or really exhausted, I still kind of push myself. Even when I’m shaky or exhausted, I still push it. I know that probably sounds bad… I’m just trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have a month where you don't try to make up for these eating episodes with extra exercise or restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a whole month without doing that.\n\nI guess I keep hoping it’s not as often as it sounds, but for the past few months there’s rarely even been a week where I don’t have at least one night where I eat way more than I meant to. I know that sounds… not good. I’m sorry. I know that probably isn’t a good way to handle it… I just get really anxious if I don’t do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to make up for these eating episodes with extra exercise or restricting your food intake at least three times a week?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is at least three times a week. I mean, I know what those are, but that hasn’t really been one of the things I do to deal with it.\n\nI mostly just feel guilty and try to be “better” the next day, if that makes sense. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you have times where you eat a large amount of food in a short period, like you described, without doing anything to try to counteract it afterwards?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I guess I usually do something afterward, even if I try to tell myself it’s just “getting back on track.”\n\nLike after one of those nights, I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school, and then I might stay out on the trail for hours or do extra yoga, even when I’m shaky and exhausted. I know that probably sounds bad. I don’t always think of it as “counteracting” in the moment, but… I guess that’s what it is.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food at night happen?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s been happening more than I’d like to admit. For the past few months, I don’t think there’s really been many weeks where it didn’t happen at least once.\n\nUsually it’s one night where I eat way more than I meant to, and then the next day I try to sort of… make up for it, I guess, by not eating at school or making myself do a really long walk or extra yoga. I know that probably isn’t the best answer, but that’s what’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: How often do you do extra exercise or restrict your food intake to make up for one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food at night?\n\nPatient: Probably… most times it happens. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but for the past few months there’s barely been a week where I didn’t have at least one of those nights, and then the next day I usually try to “fix” it somehow.\n\nLike I’ll skip eating at school, or I’ll make myself go on a really long walk, or do extra yoga even if I’m tired. I know that’s probably not a great way to handle it… I’m just trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in these extra exercises or food restrictions every time you have one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food at night?\n\nPatient: Pretty much… yes. I don’t think I let myself just move on from it.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I’ll usually tell myself I’m not allowed to have breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll try to make up for it by staying out on the trail for hours or doing extra yoga. I know that’s not ideal… I’m sorry, I’m trying to answer clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any days where you don't try to make up for one of these eating episodes with extra exercise or restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: I mean… there might be a day here or there where I don’t do it as much, but honestly, not really.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I usually tell myself I’m not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll try to make up for it by staying out on the trail for hours or doing extra yoga. Even when I’m shaky or exhausted, I still push it. I know that probably sounds bad… I’m just trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have a month where you don't try to make up for these eating episodes with extra exercise or restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a whole month without doing that.\n\nI guess I keep hoping it’s not as often as it sounds, but for the past few months there’s rarely even been a week where I don’t have at least one night where I eat way more than I meant to. I know that probably isn’t the “right” way to handle it… but I don’t usually just do nothing afterward.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have episodes of eating a large amount of food at night followed by compensatory behaviors, such as restricting food intake or extra exercise, at least three times a week for the past three months?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 25 / elementary_or_middle_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"For the past few months, there has rarely been a week without at least one night where I eat far more than I meant to, and the next day I try to make up for it by not eating at school or pushing myself through a long walk or extra yoga.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… at least once a week, yes. Honestly, for the past few months I don’t think there’s really been a week where it hasn’t happened at least one night.

I eat way more than I meant to at night, and then the next day I feel like I have to sort of… make it right. So I’ll skip eating at school, or I’ll push myself to do a really long walk or extra yoga. I’m not sure if it’s three times every week, but it’s been happening pretty regularly.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because my eating has been getting a little out of hand. Especially at night. I’ll come home after teaching, grade papers, and then I start with something normal, like leftovers… but sometimes it turns into a lot more. Like cereal, crackers, peanut butter, whatever snacks I had packed for my classroom. It keeps going until I’m really, painfully full. I know that probably sounds awful.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food at night?\n\nPatient: Pretty ashamed, honestly. I usually feel like I need to… make up for it somehow. So I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to have breakfast or lunch at school the next day, which I know probably isn’t the best.\n\nAnd sometimes I’ll go out on the trail for hours, or do extra yoga, even if I’m shaky or really tired. I tell myself it’s just being disciplined, but… it feels more like punishment, I guess. It’s more that I feel awful afterward and try to fix it in other ways.\n\nSorry, is that… is that what you meant?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to counteract the effects of your eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I know what those are, but that hasn’t really been one of the things I do to deal with it.\n\nI mostly just feel guilty and try to be “better” the next day, if that makes sense. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you have times where you eat a large amount of food in a short period, like you described, without doing anything to try to counteract it afterwards?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I guess I usually do something afterward, even if I try to tell myself it’s just “getting back on track.”\n\nLike after one of those nights, I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school, and then I might stay out on the trail for hours or do extra yoga, even when I’m shaky and exhausted. I know that probably sounds bad. I don’t always think of it as “counteracting” in the moment, but… I guess that’s what it is.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food at night happen?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s been happening more than I’d like to admit. For the past few months, I don’t think there’s really been many weeks where it didn’t happen at least once.\n\nUsually it’s one night where I eat way more than I meant to, and then the next day I try to sort of… make up for it, I guess, by not eating at school or making myself do a really long walk or extra yoga. I know that probably isn’t the best answer, but that’s what’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: How often do you do extra exercise or restrict your food intake to make up for one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food at night?\n\nPatient: Probably… most times it happens. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but for the past few months there’s barely been a week where I didn’t have at least one of those nights, and then the next day I usually try to “fix” it somehow.\n\nLike I’ll skip eating at school, or I’ll make myself go on a really long walk, or do extra yoga even if I’m tired. I know that’s probably not a great way to handle it… I’m just trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in these extra exercises or food restrictions every time you have one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food at night?\n\nPatient: Pretty much… yes. I know that’s not ideal… I’m sorry, I’m trying to answer clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any days where you don't try to make up for one of these eating episodes with extra exercise or restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: I mean… there might be a day here or there where I don’t do it as much, but honestly, not really.\n\nAfter one of those nights, I usually tell myself I’m not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll try to make up for it by staying out on the trail for hours or doing extra yoga. Even when I’m shaky or exhausted, I still push it. I know that probably sounds bad… I’m just trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have a month where you don't try to make up for these eating episodes with extra exercise or restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a whole month without doing that.\n\nI guess I keep hoping it’s not as often as it sounds, but for the past few months there’s rarely even been a week where I don’t have at least one night where I eat way more than I meant to. And then the next day I usually try to make up for it somehow… like not eating while I’m at school, or making myself go for a really long walk or do extra yoga.\n\nI’m sorry — I know that’s probably not the answer you’d want, but I’m trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to make up for these eating episodes with extra exercise or restricting your food intake every single time they happen?\n\nPatient: Pretty much, yes… I mean, I don’t like saying “every single time” because maybe there’s been a time I didn’t do it perfectly, but after one of those nights I almost always try to make up for it.\n\nUsually I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to have breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll stay out on the trail for hours, or do extra yoga, even if I’m shaky or really tired. I know that sounds… not good. I’m sorry. But yes, that’s usually what happens.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to make up for these eating episodes because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t really want to admit that, but it is mostly because I’m scared I’ll gain weight after one of those nights.\n\nSo then the next day I’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school, and I’ll try to “fix it” by staying out on the trail for hours or doing extra yoga, even when I’m shaky and exhausted. I know that probably isn’t a good way to handle it… I just get really anxious if I don’t do something.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to make up for these eating episodes with extra exercise or restricting your food intake at least three times a week?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is at least three times a week. I kind of hate admitting that, but after one of those nights, I’ll usually tell myself I’m not allowed to eat breakfast or lunch at school.\n\nAnd then I try to make up for it by staying out on the trail for hours, or doing extra yoga, even if I’m shaky and exhausted. I know that probably sounds extreme… but yes, it happens that often.\n\nDoctor: Are there times when you eat a large amount of food in a short period without trying to make up for it afterwards?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’m trying to think if there are exceptions, and maybe there are once in a while, but usually after one of those nights I do try to make up for it.\n\nI’ll tell myself I’m not allowed to have breakfast or lunch at school, and then I’ll go out on the trail for hours or do extra yoga, even if I’m shaky and exhausted. I know that probably sounds bad… but most of the time, I don’t just let it happen without doing something after.\n\nDoctor: Are there any weeks where you have an episode of eating a large amount of food at night without then trying to make up for it the next day?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… I don’t think there are many weeks like that, no.\n\nUsually if I eat a lot at night, the next day I feel like I have to do something to balance it out.     "psychosocial_stressor": null,
```json
    "additional_requirements": [
      "Onset of symptoms occurred after the patient had reached an age where they could be expected to understand the consequences of their actions",
      "The patient's behavior is not better explained by another mental disorder"
    ]
  }
}
```
